#     sampled) and cuts 820 vertices to 49.
# Together these took the envelope from 11.25 s to a small fraction of a second,
# with a byte-identical envelope and an identical plan.
# --- Merged presentation of the derived risk field -------------------------
# Twenty-one cells times three tiers is sixty-three outlines. Around the
# provincial capitals they overlap, and the result is a smear of translucent fills
# with three sets of crossing outlines rather than a readable hazard. The tiers
# are therefore dissolved into connected components on the way out.
#
# This is presentation only, and the distinction matters. Every position still
# comes from `centre_at`; the union is built from the very geometry `shapes()`
# returns; and `swept()` still envelopes each cell separately. The avoidance
# envelope is consequently a superset of whatever is drawn, so merging can never
# hide an area the planner refuses to enter — it only stops drawing the seams.
# Ties go to the more severe kind. The vocabulary is now the *extreme* one --
# a point observation only mints geometry when it names a genuinely hazardous
# condition, see ``_extreme_kind``.
```

### L290

```
# --- What the point feed is allowed to turn into geometry ------------------
# An observation is a reading at one place. Turning it into an area is a claim
# this system makes about airspace, and the claim has to be earned, so only an
# *extreme* reading mints one:
#
#   * a *condition* the provider itself classifies as extreme: 暴雨 / 雷暴.
#     The adapter maps its own code space onto one vocabulary
#     (``conditionClass``, see ``weather_sources``) -- this module never reads a
#     raw provider code, because the two providers publish colliding integers.
#   * gust >= 17 m/s (8 级风), visibility < 1000 m, or >= 8 mm of rain in the
#     hour -- the same figures the retired "severe" branch used.
#
# Anything milder is **reported and nothing else**: it stays in
# ``snapshot.observations`` for the operator, and mints no polygon at all.
#
# Why this replaced per-observation discs (measured 2026-09-30, live snapshot,
# seed 42): the old rule gave every city that cleared a mild threshold
# (gust >= 12, precipitation >= 1 mm, visibility < 5000 m) an 18 km field and a
# 12 km caution ring centred on the city, and `envelope()` then unioned
# ``ban | caution`` into the routing obstacle set. Eleven cities qualified, not
# one had a prohibition core, and 2488 flights were closed with their own pad
# inside a *caution* ring -- "enter with care" had become "you may not leave".
# Zero detours were produced by a radar channel that carried 528 km2 of >=35 dBZ
# echo. Discs pinned to cities are also the one shape the project forbids
# elsewhere ("inventing a buffer would put unverified boundary data on the map").
#
# The hazard area an operator should actually see is the one the radar measured.
# These constants only cover the case the radar cannot express: an extreme
# reading at a station the mosaic has not resolved yet.
# 0.11.15（K-4）：与走廊判据**同一个键**（`config/rules-v1.json::operations.gustMps`）。
# 原来这里是 17.0，而走廊判据是 10 —— 于是"判了 10 条不可飞的走廊"在地图上找不出对应
# 风险区。现在两边同源，改阈值只改配置文件。
```

### L333

```
# **几何阈值**：哪一块空域要画成"禁止进入"的内核。
#
# 它读的键是 `operations.extremeGustMps`（17.0），**不是**规则阈值
# `operations.gustMps`（11.5）—— 这两个数故意不同，理由写在配置文件里那段
# `extremeGustRationale` 上：0.11.15 第一版把两者统一到 11.5，结果长春 11.83 也成了
# 禁止内核，飞行计划绕不开，**关闭班次从 1430 涨到 4289（55%）**，等于好天气停飞一半。
#
# 这正是项目里那条老规矩：地图几何阈值 ≠ 规则阈值，允许不同；
# 「对齐」不等于「改成同一个数」，要补的是**关系声明**。
```

### L345

```
# 禁止内核半径。The one shape an extreme reading earns is a **compact
# prohibition disc** at the station, not a ring plus a core: one reading, one
# enforcement level. 6 km is urban-thunderstorm scale and keeps the footprint
# from swallowing a whole city, which is what an 18 km disc did.
```

### L351

```
# 阈值的**机器可读副本**，只给界面引用。The numbers above are used to decide;
# this table exists so the operator's weather briefing can state the threshold
# that produced a hazard area instead of printing a placeholder. Two hard-coded
# copies would drift, so the briefing reads this and nothing else -- the test
# `test_threshold_table_matches_constants` pins them together.
```

### L370

```
# --- 低能见度（雾）派生层：与雷达回波同构的第二条气象通道 --------------------
#
# 为什么它必须是一条**独立通道**，而不是"又一种极端读数"。
#
# 在此之前，能见度 < 1000 m 走 ``_extreme_kind`` → ``point_risk_shapes``，在站心生成
# 一个 6 km 的禁止内核。而 ``city_pads(city)[0]`` **就是城区代表中心**，所以那个内核
# 把枢纽包在里面 —— 该城所有班次连起飞都不可能，只能被拒。实测 seed 42 有 673 班
# ``WEATHER_BLOCKED``，成因即此。屏幕上却看不出为什么：那 6 km 圆盘被并进了同一片
# 暖色「气象风险区」，在三省全域视图下只有十几像素。用户原话：
# 「刚开始有几个标黄的路径不知道原因关闭」。
#
# 所以本层要同时做两件事：**让它看得见**（独立图层、独立颜色族）、**让它说得清**
# （两条阈值、每城一个缓冲圆、规则数字随结果发布）。拒绝本身**不变** —— 600 m 能见度
# 低于本系统自己的禁飞阈值，那座城不该放行；要修的是"不知道为什么"。
#
# 两档与雷达回波**同构**（这是用户选的口径：「原理其实是一样的」）：
#   深灰 = 低于禁飞阈值（hard）↔ 雷达 ≥45 dBZ 内核（禁止进入）
#   浅灰 = 能见度偏低（soft）  ↔ 雷达 35–45 dBZ 场（须绕飞，可擦边）
#
# 浅灰档带一条**关键豁免**，它是这条通道能不能存在的前提：``rules-v1.json`` 把
# 3 km 能见度判为 ``CAUTION``（警戒，扣 12 分），不是 ``BAN``。让一个"本系统自己只判
# 警戒"的读数去**拒绝**整座城，正是把数归给它没被算过的那个原因。更具体地说，
# 2026-09-30 已经量过一次这个错误：轻度过境被做成站心圆盘并并入航路障碍集合后，
# **2488 班**因为"自己的停机坪落在自己的警戒环里"而被关（当时是 18 km 场 + 12 km
# 环）。所以浅灰档只**掰弯航路**：起降点自己落在这个圆里时，这个圆对该班次不作约束。
# 回归判据见 ``test_mildly_adverse_weather_mints_no_geometry_and_no_obstacle``
# （它守的是暖色风险层）与 ``test_fog_caution_never_refuses_a_city_that_sits_inside_it``
# （它守的是本条新通道 —— 同一个缺陷类，第二个可能出现的地方）。
#
# 半径是**声明值，不是测量值**：一次站点读数只给一个点，给不了范围。取值的唯一标准是
# "三省全域视图上看得见、又不至于吞掉整座城"，且两条半径随结果发布以便核对 ——
# 宣称一个范围而不给数字，读者就无从复核（同 ``_radar_rule_block``）。
```

### L403

```
# 深灰档**沿用既有的内核半径**，不新造一个数：这样"改造前后深灰档对 hard 集合的贡献"
# 可以逐步几何对照，把"看得见的改动"与"行为改动"分离开（前者是本次目标，后者不是）。
```

### L432

```
# 配置缺失/损坏时 ``load_spec`` 自己会回落到内置默认；这里再兜一层，
# 是为了让"读不到配置"表现为"用声明过的默认值"，而不是让整条通道消失。
```

### L629

```
# A member's geometry must be attributed to the component it landed *in*, not
# carried whole. Until the band became a chain of clusters one cell was one
# polygon, so a cell touched exactly one component and the two were the same
# thing. A chained band is a single cell spanning ~20 components, and listing
# that cell as a member of every one of them replicated its id once per region:
# measured 38 member slots against 20 drawable cells at minute 0. The client
# then rendered the same source id eighteen times, and the promise that a merged
# area can be traced back to the cells behind it stopped meaning anything --
# every region claimed to be the whole band.
```

### L644

```
# A cell whose field clips to nothing here is not a member, even when
# its core or ring still grazes the component.
```

### L647

```
# A cell's core has to be attributed to the component its *field* landed in.
# Until the band became a chain of clusters one cell was always one polygon,
# so unioning every member's whole ban was harmless. A chained band is a
# single cell whose ban is a MultiPolygon of ~20 parts spread up to 1.14 Mm
# apart, and the union therefore handed every region all ~20 cores: measured
# 142,047,519,714 m^2 of core outside its own field, and a core whose nearest
# approach to the region's field was 1,135,977 m. The visible effect is a
# no-fly patch painted far from the cloud it belongs to, so clipping each
# part to the region's field is what makes the drawn tiers mean what they say.
```

### L657

```
# The caution tier is rebuilt for the merged region rather than unioned cell
# by cell. Two cells only merge because their fields overlap, and then one
# cell's core can land inside another cell's caution band — unioning the
# bands would paint amber over a core, which is the more severe statement.
# Rebuilding it as "the cells' own outer envelope minus the merged cores"
# keeps the two tiers disjoint, and it reduces to each source's original
# definition: a ring cell's outer envelope is its field buffered by 900 m,
# a live observation's is the field itself (its caution is already the field
# minus the core, not a buffered ring).
```

### L719

```
# Ties go to the more severe kind, so a clump holding one storm cell is not
# relabelled as rain just because rain happens to have the same count.
```

### L728

```
# A region made only of a spanning cell's clusters. It is named after the
# cell it comes from plus a cluster suffix, so it stays distinguishable from
# the cell's other clusters and from any point cell of the same name.
```

### L761

```
# Power-law falloff: amplitude ~ 1/order^0.9. Order 2 dominates (the main
# asymmetry), higher orders add roughness. A floor keeps the outline from
# becoming noise-thin at high order.
```

### L766

```
# Anisotropy: real cells are rarely round. The stretch is applied along a
# per-cell axis, so elongation direction differs from cell to cell.
```

### L806

```
# A narrow harmonic set gets the gentler roughness; see the constant's note.
# The two are not interchangeable: the same amplitude on two lobes is a much
# sharper turn than on eight, because there is no neighbour lobe to share the
# deflection with.
```

### L812

```
# Sample density has to satisfy two independent requirements, and the vertex
# count is the larger of the two.
#
# (1) Nyquist on the highest order present. A shape built from orders 2-3
#     turns at most 3 times around the circle, so 96 points puts ~30 samples
#     on every lobe -- far past the point of information, and the long runs of
#     near-collinear vertices are what a later `simplify()` collapses. The
#     core's outline was being cut from 96 vertices to 19 that way, and at 19
#     points the remaining turns measured 47.5 degrees.
#
# (2) A curvature budget. Nyquist alone is not sufficient, and the failure it
#     misses is real: measured on seed 42, WX-1-08 (order up to 9, 96
#     vertices) still carried a 47.85-degree convex cusp, and WX-1-03 (order
#     up to 6, 72 vertices) carried 46.88 degrees. Both sit above the
#     45-degree line that separates "a cloud edge" from "a spike". The reason
#     is that the angular deflection at a vertex is not set by the order
#     count but by the *slope* of the radius function, and `stretch` multiplies
#     that slope: with `stretch` s the turn at the long-axis ends scales by
#     about s, so an s of 1.44 adds ~44% to the sharpest turn. This is why the
#     violating vertices landed at index vertices//2 -- the stretched ends.
#
#     The budget is the first derivative of the radius divided by the arc
#     step. Summing |d(r*scale)/dtheta| over the harmonics bounds the slope,
#     and the turn at a vertex cannot exceed slope * (2*pi/vertices) for the
#     tangential-plus-radial construction used below.
#
#     MEASURED CORRECTION (see tools/benches/_bench_innovations.py, section 1).
#     That bound is not sufficient, and leaving it as the only guard left four
#     cells per 134 with a 46-56 degree turn -- worst 55.78 degrees at seed 42,
#     which is a spike by exactly the criterion this budget exists to enforce.
#     The reason is that the turn at a vertex is governed by the *second*
#     derivative (curvature), not the first: a sample sitting on an arc that
#     is locally flat but strongly curved turns a lot even where the slope is
#     small. The first-derivative bound therefore underestimates, by a factor
#     measured at 6-9x on the offending cells, so the budget term computed
#     14-16 vertices where 96-144 were needed and never once bound.
#
#     Rather than fit a fudge factor to four cells, the vertex count is now
#     refined by measuring the real turn and raising the count until the limit
#     holds. The analytic budget is kept as the starting point (it is cheap
#     and always a lower bound); the loop is what makes the guarantee true.
```

### L854

```
# The stretched ends carry the extra turn; the mean of s and 1/s bounds how
# much the elongation may amplify the slope for a fixed perimeter.
```

### L861

```
# Sample half a step off the axis. With an even vertex count, index
# ``vertices//2`` lands at exactly theta = pi, which is the +x end of the
# ellipse *before* rotation -- the stretched long-axis endpoint, where the
# curvature is highest. Measured on seed 42: the two remaining spikes sat at
# index/total = 0.50 and 0.51, i.e. precisely there, at 46.88 and 46.13
# degrees. Shifting the sample grid by half a step moves the nearest vertex
# away from the extreme and lets the turn be shared between two samples.
#
# This is a sampling fix, not a shape fix: the continuous curve is unchanged,
# and the outline is still generated from exactly the same harmonics, stretch
# and radius. Only where the vertices are placed along it moves.
```

### L879

```
# Sum of harmonics in *angle* (never in vertex index -- driving them
# by the index is what produced the cusped flower).
```

### L883

```
# Normalise so the mean radius stays ~= radius and the modulation is
# a bounded fraction of it rather than growing with the count.
```

### L893

```
# Refine by measurement. The analytic budget above bounds the *slope*, and the
# turn at a vertex is governed by curvature, so it under-predicts -- measured
# at 6-9x on the cells it was supposed to protect. The count is therefore
# raised until the sampled turn actually satisfies the limit. This consumes no
# randomness, so a cell's outline still depends only on its own parameters.
#
# The step is a computed jump, not a nudge. Subdividing an arc lowers the
# turn at a vertex roughly in proportion to the step length, so requesting
# `vertices * turn/limit` samples moves straight to the neighbourhood of the
# answer; the loop then only cleans up the error in that estimate. A fixed
# 1.5x growth needs six rounds to cover a 10x gap and ran into the cap before
# converging -- measured, that left three cells still over the limit.
#
# The cap is raised for this reason: 128 was tuned when the analytic budget
# alone set the count, but the cells that need correcting are the ones with
# the highest curvature, and they need the extra room. The cap still exists,
# so a pathological parameter draw cannot cost unbounded vertices; a cell that
# reaches it is reported by _bench_innovations.py rather than passing quietly.
```

### L920

```
# No simplify here. The shared SIMPLIFY_TOLERANCE (120 m) is tuned for the
# large band polygons, and on a 6-10 km cell it removed enough vertices to
# leave 90-degree corners -- measured worst exterior turn of exactly 90.0
# degrees, i.e. right angles on what is supposed to be a cloud edge. The
# outline is returned unsimplified and each consumer applies its own
# tolerance at the size it actually works with: `shapes` keeps the full
# outline because it is drawn, while the swept envelope hulls it first (see
# `_outline_hull_coords`) and never sees the vertex count at all.
```

### L1020

```
# The core is nudged off centre so it does not read as a drawn ring. Shared by the
# core and the inner edge of the caution ring, which must coincide.
```

### L1128

```
# The clusters are drawn from their own generator, seeded off a fixed value,
# so every run produces the same chain: an operator who reloads sees the same
# weather, and a plan built at t=0 still matches the picture at t=200. Large
# masses get fewer, chunkier harmonics than small ones so a 30 km cluster does
# not read as a spiky ball.
```

### L1139

```
# Births sit on an even stride with jitter -- see `BAND_CLUSTER_BIRTH_SPAN_MIN`
# for why an independent draw was rejected. The stride is what keeps the live
# count steady; the jitter is what keeps it from looking mechanical.
#
# The lead bias makes the segment nearest the leading edge on average the
# youngest, because a front advances. It is small relative to the stride so
# segment age does not become a function of position.
```

### L1148

```
# The slot grid is inset by one jitter so no cluster's birth can land outside
# the intended span: measured before this, the first and last slots reached
# -425 and +511 against a span of +/-420, which put one segment's whole life
# before the run and left the tail thinner than the stride predicted.
```

### L1161

```
# A cluster waxes and wanes around its base radius; the direction of the
# phase is its own, so neighbouring segments do not breathe in unison.
```

### L1228

```
# Maps id(cluster) -> the cluster dict. The memo below is keyed only on hashable
# values, so the dict is reached through this side table instead of being passed
# in. Entries are inserted on first use and dropped by `_forget_outlines`.
```

### L1267

```
# The side table was cleared underneath a live memo (a new network was
# built). Returning "nothing here" matches what a cluster that has been
# dropped from the model should report, and the caller's next call
# re-registers the cluster.
```

### L1326

```
# Each cell gets its own life history. Birth is spread across the run
# so echoes appear and disappear instead of the whole field switching
# on at t=0 and off together; a share is already mature at t=0.
```

### L1335

```
# A per-cell shape seed. This is the whole reason two cells drawn from
# the same generator look different rather than like scaled copies.
```

### L1339

```
# The shape parameters are drawn once and stored, not re-drawn per
# frame: the cell's silhouette has to be stable over time while its
# *size* changes, otherwise it would flicker between unrelated shapes
# instead of evolving. `_core_outline` reads the same dict so the core
# stays recognisably part of the same cloud.
```

### L1356

```
# The band is kept as a list of *separate* clusters rather than one unioned
# polygon, so each segment can run its own life cycle (see `_band_clusters`).
# `polygon` is the t=0 union of whatever is alive then, which is what the
# `_spans_regions` check and any static analysis of the cell expect; the
# per-minute outline is rebuilt by `shapes()`.
```

### L1363

```
# The outline memo is keyed on `id(cluster)`, so it must be reset when a new
# network is built: a freed dict's id can be reused by a later allocation
# and the memo would then hand back the *old* cluster's outline.
```

### L1393

```
# Direction: mostly along the parent's motion, with a lateral component,
# which is the geometry of the classic left/right split.
```

### L1402

```
# `parent` records the lineage. Without it a daughter is only
# distinguishable by its `-D1` id suffix, so any analysis of split
# behaviour ("does the pair separate?", "how many splits per seed?")
# has to pattern-match on a string instead of reading a field.
```

### L1452

```
# Fixed-outline cells (the band) carry no life cycle: the band is a
# synoptic feature sustained by the cold vortex, with a lifetime of
# days, not the tens of minutes of a single convective cell. The cold
# vortex over Northeast China persists 3-4 days on average, so over an
# eight-hour timeline it is effectively steady (CMA; 6 月平均活动
# 20.6 天).
```

### L1471

```
# A cell that never grows past its seed radius would be invisible at
# birth; the floor keeps the earliest stage a small but real echo, which
# matches the "first echo appears, then intensifies" observation.
```

### L1493

```
# Two motions compose, and they are different in kind:
#
# 1. The whole front advances, expressed by `drift(cell,t)`. That is
#    the synoptic motion of the cold vortex steering the line and it
#    applies to every segment equally.
# 2. Each segment runs its own life cycle -- thickening, thinning,
#    then dropping out of the chain -- expressed by
#    `_band_cluster_outlines`. That is what makes the band read as
#    rain *passing* rather than a stencil sliding across the map.
#
# Every cluster is translated by the same offset and then drawn at
# its own radius, so the two compose without either cancelling the
# other. The outline is rebuilt per minute rather than stored, so a
# growing segment's edge develops instead of being a scaled
# photograph of its t=0 self.
```

### L1517

```
# The whole front has dissipated. Dropping the cell entirely is
# what keeps the chain from leaving empty geometry behind for the
# dissolve step to walk, and it is a real state: a front does end.
```

### L1524

```
# The core is a shrunken copy of each segment, not a negative buffer.
# `field.buffer(-BAND_CORE_INSET_M)` was producing 53 exterior turns
# above 45 degrees, worst 150.5, with edges down to 92 m: a negative
# buffer on a large polygon whose edge carries roughness erodes
# unevenly, and where two eroded fronts meet they leave cusps. That is
# the same failure the per-cell core had, and it has the same fix --
# rebuild from the low-order shape and scale it, so the core keeps the
# segment's overall form without inheriting the roughness that
# erosion turns into spikes.
```

### L1536

```
# A dissipated cell simply is not there. Dropping it here (rather than
# shrinking it to nothing) is what makes the map gain and lose
# outlines over the run; `ban`/`caution` of a vanished cell would
# otherwise linger as empty geometry that the dissolve step still has
# to walk.
```

### L1545

```
# The shape parameters are the cell's own and do not change, but the
# outline is regenerated at the *current* radius, so a growing cell is
# not a scaled photograph of itself -- its edge develops as the cell
# evolves, which is what a real echo does. Determinism holds: same seed,
# same minute, same polygon, so a plan and the picture always agree.
```

### L1551

```
# The no-fly core is a smaller copy of the same shape, rebuilt from the
# low-order harmonics only. See `_core_outline` for why it is not a
# scaled copy of the detailed outline.
```

### L1555

```
# The caution tier is <the field grown by CAUTION_BUFFER_M> minus the
# core, both built from low-order shapes.
#
# It used to be `field.buffer(900)` minus a core derived from the
# *detailed* outline. Two smooth-but-unrelated outlines that run nearly
# parallel leave a sliver where they cross, and `difference` keeps that
# sliver: measured 177.4 and 166.0 degree corners on WX-3-06, i.e. a
# zero-width spike, which is a rendering artefact and not a piece of
# airspace. Building both edges from the same low-order shape makes them
# concentric by construction, so the difference has no crossing to leave
# a sliver at.
```

### L1567

```
# Tolerance is proportional to the cell, never the shared figure. The
# shared SIMPLIFY_TOLERANCE (120 m) is tuned for the ~30 km band
# polygons; on a 5 km core it consumed almost the whole outline and
# left 19 vertices whose turns measured 47.5 degrees.
#
# The percentage is 1, not 5, because the outline's vertex spacing is
# now chosen from its own harmonic content (see `_irregular_outline`),
# so there is little redundancy left to remove. Simplification at this
# point should only drop sub-metre duplicates, and `MIN_CORE_VERTICES`
# guards the case where it does more than that: a core that comes back
# below the floor is kept unsimplified, because a coarse core is a
# geometry defect while a slightly redundant one is free.
```

### L1590

```
# Every point in an interval is within 150 seconds of a sample, so the
# per-cell allowance bounds translation between samples (<=6.7 m/s). The
# envelope covers both the no-fly core and the caution band of every cell.
#
# Keyed on the *binned* tick window, and every caller must route through
# the binning entry point below. The planner calls ``envelope`` with raw
# float minutes (``departure + duration + maxExtra``), which produced
# 13411 distinct float pairs over 13419 calls -- one unique key per call,
# so a cache on the raw arguments could never hit. Binning first gives
# ~588 distinct windows over the plan.
#
# An older note here claimed the binning yielded a 92.3% hit rate
# (13,419 calls -> 1,035 computations, "envelope 11.25 s -> 5.60 s").
# That number does not survive being measured and must not be quoted:
# the planner issues calls in *sorted departure order*, so a window is
# usually first requested long before it repeats, and only ~59.8% of
# calls hit on a real plan. The cache is also instance-scoped, so a
# freshly built ``NetworkWeather`` starts cold. Measured on 08-16:
# 588 distinct windows across 108,640 planner calls; a probe that asked
# for windows out of order saw a 0.0% hit rate at ~110 ms per miss.
#
# The practical consequence is that cost per *miss* dominates, not the
# hit rate -- which is why the band's swept hull had to stop extending
# all 820 outline vertices (see ``_outline_hull_coords``). See the note
# by ``SWEPT_BAND_PAD_M`` for why the window may not simply be widened.
```

### L1621

```
# A cell that does not exist at any minute in this window cannot
# constrain a flight that only crosses the window, so it contributes
# no envelope. Without this, every cell's full track would be carved
# out of the airspace for the entire eight hours even though most of
# them are only alive for an hour -- the map would show open sky while
# the planner refused to use it.
```

### L1632

```
# The buffer must cover the cell at its *largest* within this window,
# not at its size right now. A cell that grows after the flight is
# planned would otherwise leave the flight inside it -- the same class
# of error as the band that routing missed entirely. `sweptPad` is
# derived from the cell's nominal (peak) radius, so it already bounds
# the life cycle; the growth factor is added explicitly to be sure.
```

### L1689

```
# The set that has to be enveloped is not `{one outline + d_t}` any more, and
# assuming it were would make the envelope too small. Each cluster now changes
# radius over the window, so the shape at tick A differs from the shape at
# tick B and no single outline can stand in for both: translating a mid-life
# outline would under-cover the ticks where a segment is near its maximum,
# and a too-small envelope is precisely the defect that lets a trajectory
# thread the band. So the hull is taken over the cluster outlines *sampled
# across the window* as well as across the drift offsets.
#
# There used to be a prune here that kept only the drift offsets sitting on
# the drift hull, guarded by `len(offsets) > 24`. It was a no-op on real
# windows and is gone rather than fixed: at span 14 there are 15 offsets, so
# the guard never fired, and its support test ("x *or* y equals a hull
# vertex coordinate") is loose enough that 15 of 15 survived even when it
# did fire. It could not help anyway -- the Minkowski step below already
# reduces the drift set to its hull vertices, which is exactly what the
# prune was trying to approximate.
#
# `_swept_window` caches per 5-minute window, so this runs once per window
# rather than once per flight, and the per-cluster hull is small.
#
# The zero offset is *already* in `offsets` whenever the window contains
# tick 0 (``self.drift`` returns exactly ``(0.0, 0.0)`` there), and the
# hull below is built from `offset + outline` pairs, so a missing origin
# would only shrink the envelope by the outline's own extent. Adding it
# must therefore append a *tuple*: `offsets.extend((0.0, 0.0))` appends
# two bare floats into a list of pairs and the unpack below then raises
# `TypeError: cannot unpack non-iterable float object`.
```

### L1718

```
# Hull each side *first*, then sum the two hulls.
#
# ``hull(A + B) == hull(hull(A) + hull(B))`` is exact for finite point sets
# -- verified against the direct computation on eight real windows with a
# worst-case area difference of 0.000 km^2 -- and it reduces the point set
# from ~15 x ~4000 = 60,000 raw sums to |verts(hull A)| x |verts(hull B)|.
# The direction of any error matters, so both hulls are taken with
# ``MultiPoint`` rather than by dropping points: too small an envelope lets
# a trajectory thread the band, which is the defect this function exists to
# prevent, and the identity says hulling cannot shrink the result.
#
# KNOWN LIMITATION -- this bridges the gaps between segments. Pooling every
# cluster's coordinates before hulling makes a convex hull of the whole
# chain, which is a blob spanning the chain, so the envelope covers airspace
# between segments that the band itself leaves open. That was measured
# rather than assumed: on eight real windows the bridged envelope is
# 268,011 km^2 against 137,881 km^2 for a per-cluster union, so **48.6% of
# what this returns is not under any cloud**.
#
# It is kept deliberately, because the operational effect was measured as
# nil while the alternative was measured as slower:
#   * testing every point of all 7760 planned trajectories against the
#     windowed bridging regions found **0 points** inside one -- no flight
#     is routed through a bridged corridor, so the extra area changes no
#     decision (`tools/probes/_probe_band_bridging.py`);
#   * the per-cluster construction that removes the bridging measured
#     92 s per plan against 66 s for this one, a 39% regression, and the
#     cause was NOT the hull (per-window it is actually faster) nor the
#     envelope's part count -- so the regression is unexplained, and
#     shipping an unexplained 39% cost for a provably inert difference is
#     the wrong trade.
# If trajectories are ever routed through a bridged corridor, this is the
# line to revisit, and `_probe_band_bridging.py` is the test that will say so.
```

### L1757

```
# Nothing alive anywhere in this window: the front has dissipated, so
# the envelope is empty rather than a stale shape.
```

### L1760

```
# ``convex_hull`` of a point set is a Polygon, but of a single point or a
# degenerate collinear set it is a Point or LineString, which has no
# ``exterior``. Read the coordinates through the right attribute instead
# of assuming, or a one-offset window would raise here.
```

### L1772

```
# Bin *here*, at the boundary, so the cache key is the binned window.
# Passing raw floats into the cached function made every key unique (13411
# raw pairs over 13419 calls) and the cache useless; it is the binned pair
# that repeats, because departure times come from a fixed set of waves.
```

### L1779

```
# Clipped before dissolving rather than after: intersection distributes
# over union, so the result is identical and clipping the small per-cell
# shapes is much cheaper than clipping one three-province blob.
```

### L1876

```
# Reported, never drawn: the reading stays in `observations` so the
# operator can see it, and mints no polygon.
```

### L1880

```
# 能见度现在由**独立的两档通道**承载（``fog_tiers``），不在这条路径上出几何。
# 理由不是整洁，是**档位数目不同**：这里"一次读数、一个禁止内核"，
# 而雾区是两条阈值、两档，且偏低那一档**不允许拒绝整座城**。
# 而这条路径的核在站心、半径 6 km，``city_pads(city)[0]`` 又正是城区代表
# 中心 —— 也就是说这里造出来的核**必然包含枢纽**，于是"只能拒绝，不能只掰弯"。
# 把一条本系统自己只判 ``CAUTION`` 的读数（3 km 能见度扣 12 分）交给一条
# 只能拒绝的通道，就是把数归给它没被算过的那个原因。
# ``_extreme_kind`` 保留 ``LOW_VISIBILITY`` 这个分支：它仍是对"这个站现在
# 是什么天气"的正确回答，只是不再由本函数兑现成几何。
```

### L1891

```
# quad_segs=16 on a 6 km disc is already smooth, so only the tolerance
# simplify is applied: it keeps the same area while shedding redundant
# vertices on the arc.
```

### L1964

```
# The derived-risk label names the provider that actually produced the
# snapshot, so a QWeather-driven field is never labelled as Open-Meteo.
```

### L1974

```
# A merged area covering several observation sites reports all of
# their thresholds, so the derivation stays traceable per site.
```

### L2046

```
# Independent of the window on purpose: the source is a measurement, not
# a forecast trajectory, so there is no honest way to vary it by minute.
```

### L410

```
``(偏低档阈值, 禁飞档阈值)``，**从 SFI 规则配置读**，这里不另写一份。

    ``config/rules-v1.json`` 的 ``visibilityM``（direction ``low``，warn 5000 /
    ban 1000）已经是本系统"多少能见度算危险"的权威口径：SFI 按它扣分、
    ``LEVEL_LABELS_CN`` 按它给等级。雾区几何若自带一份数字，屏幕上就会出现
    "评分说这是警戒、地图说这是禁区"两个互相矛盾的结论，而两边各自都算对了 ——
    即 M79（同一个量有两份副本时，两份各自被钉过也不会报警）。

    边界取**严格小于**，与 ``_extreme_kind`` 一致（``visibility == 1000`` 不算禁飞档，
    但算偏低档）。``rules.FactorRule.banned`` 用的是 ``<=``，两处对"正好等于阈值"的
    读法不同；这里选择与既有 ``_extreme_kind`` 一致的那一种，并在
    ``test_fog_thresholds_follow_the_sfi_rule_config`` 里把两件事同时钉住：
    阈值确实来自配置，且"正好等于"归偏低档。
```

### L439

```
一个站心缓冲圆，**与既有的极端内核同一种造法**。

    沿用 ``point_risk_shapes`` 的 ``quad_segs=16`` + ``simplify(SIMPLIFY_TOLERANCE)``：
    深灰档用同一个半径、同一种造法，才能断言"改造只动了可见性与档位，没动禁止几何"。
```

### L448

```
Per-city two-tier low-visibility discs, in **projected metres**.

    Returns ``[(cell, caution, ban), ...]`` -- one row per city whose visibility
    clears at least one tier. Shape of the three elements:

      * ``caution`` -- the annulus **outside** ``ban`` (``disc(caution) - disc(ban)``),
        so the milder tier never paints over the deeper one. This is the same
        reconstruction the V1.5 risk merge had to do (`CAUTION` rebuilt as
        ``{FIELD ∪ CAUTION} - BAN``); the measured cost of unioning the tiers
        instead is in that decision's note.
      * ``ban`` -- the inner prohibition disc, or an empty collection.
      * ``cell`` -- the reading itself plus the tier name and the rule numbers, so
        the layer can be labelled per city and a reader can check the thresholds
        that cut it.

    A city below the ban threshold appears here with **both** tiers; a city merely
    below the caution threshold appears with ``ban`` empty. Anything at or above
    the caution threshold mints **nothing** -- reported in ``observations``, never
    drawn (the same disposition ``point_risk_shapes`` documents, and the reason the
    guard against the 2488 closures stays green).

    This reads visibility **directly** rather than going through
    ``_extreme_kind``, and that is deliberate: ``_extreme_kind`` reports one
    condition per site, so a foggy city that is *also* thundering is labelled
    ``THUNDERSTORM`` and its visibility would be dropped. The two hazards are
    independent facts about the same place and both belong on the map.
```

### L513

```
雾区两档的**数字**，随每一份结果发布。

    与 ``_radar_rule_block`` 同一条理由：界面早就能写「能见度低于禁飞阈值＝禁止进入」，
    但读者无从核对"低于多少""半径多大"。断言不是证据 —— 同一份求值多带几个数字，
    不新增任何推导、不新增第二条规则。
```

### L540

```
GeoJSON for a tier, with "no such tier" stated as an empty polygon.

    ``mapping()`` on an empty geometry returns ``None``, and ``None`` is not a
    geometry: a renderer that does ``d.ban.coordinates`` throws, and a consumer
    that treats ``None`` as "this region has no core" cannot tell it apart from
    "this region's core was dropped by a bug". Low visibility without strong gusts
    is a real, routine state — it produces a caution ring with no red centre — so
    the payload has to be able to say that in a value the drawing code understands.

    An empty ``Polygon`` is that value: valid GeoJSON, zero area, and safe to ask
    for ``.coordinates``. Only ``field`` is required to be areal, because a region
    with no drawn area would have nothing to render at all.
```

### L566

```
Vertices of the convex hull of every polygon in ``geometry``.

    Used by the band's swept envelope. Only a point on the outline's convex hull
    can be extremal in the swept hull, and the swept set is a union of translates
    of the outline, so hulling first loses nothing while cutting the vertex count
    sharply: for the 20-cluster chain this is 820 vertices down to 49.

    Every component must be included, not just the largest. The small clusters are
    exactly what bound the gaps the band leaves open, so dropping them would shrink
    the envelope over the passable corridors.
```

### L585

```
Vertices of a convex hull, whatever dimension it turned out to be.

    ``MultiPoint(...).convex_hull`` returns a ``Polygon`` for a genuinely
    two-dimensional point set, but a ``Point`` for one point and a ``LineString``
    for a collinear set. Reading ``.exterior`` on those raises ``AttributeError``,
    which would turn a degenerate-but-legal window into a crash. Both degenerate
    cases are reachable here: a window whose clusters are all dead leaves one
    offset, and a drift path that happens to be straight leaves collinear ones.
```

### L608

```
Group ``(cell, field, ban, caution)`` tuples into merged areas.

    Regions are the connected components of the *field* tier, because the field is
    the outermost of the three and therefore contains every core and ring that
    belongs to it. Cores and caution bands are then taken from the member cells
    directly rather than by clipping the dissolved tiers against the region: a
    caution band deliberately extends past its field, and clipping it to the region
    outline would shave off exactly the margin that makes it a caution band.

    Both of those statements are about a region whose members are all one cell deep,
    and that is the case the function has to protect. It does so by intersecting
    every collected core with the region's own field and by *splitting* a member
    cell's geometry across the components it spans -- see the notes by ``ban`` and
    ``members`` below for what each guarded against.
```

### L694

```
Stable-ish name and provenance for a merged area.

    The anchor is the alphabetically first member, which keeps the identifier
    recognisable across minutes for as long as the clump keeps that cell. The full
    member list travels with the record, so a merged area can always be traced back
    to the individual cells — and through them to `centre_at` and the seed — even
    though the client only ever sees one outline.

    A cell that spans several regions is a member of each of them, and the two kinds
    of member need different labels. A point cell is fully identified by its id, and
    the id appears in exactly one region. The band is one cell covering a ~1,200 km
    chain of clusters, so it appears in every region along that chain, and its plain
    id would say nothing about *which* cluster the region is -- eighteen regions
    would all report the identical source. A spanning cell therefore contributes one
    ``<id>::CLUSTER`` entry per region instead: still traceable to the cell, and
    still telling one region apart from another. ``memberCount`` counts these
    per-region entries, which is what the record is describing.
```

### L744

```
Whether one cell's own shape covers more than one area.

    Only a chained cell does. It is the reason a per-region cluster label exists.
```

### L752

```
Draw the shape parameters for one cell.

    Kept separate from the geometry so a cell's *core* can be rebuilt from the
    same shape at lower detail (see ``_core_outline``): the two outlines must be
    recognisably the same cloud, which only holds if they share these draws.
```

### L774

```
A closed, irregular, non-circular outline around ``(cx,cy)``.

    Why this exists: the previous outline was ``Point.buffer(r)`` for the band
    clusters and a three-harmonic ring for the city cells. Both read as
    *manufactured* -- a buffered point is a near-perfect circle, and three
    low harmonics with a shared phase give every cell the same soft scallop. Real
    radar echoes are none of that. A convective cell is a lumpy, asymmetric blob
    whose edge is set by the gust front on one side and the inflow on the other,
    and no two cells in a radar image have the same silhouette.

    The construction is the standard one for synthetic cloud fields: sum a set of
    harmonics whose amplitudes fall off as a power law, each with its own phase
    drawn per cell, and modulate the radius by the result. A power-law amplitude
    falloff (rather than equal amplitudes) is what makes the edge read as
    "large-scale distortion + fine roughness" instead of a star; it is the same
    spectral shape that gives natural coastlines their look.

    Crucially every parameter that can vary does vary per cell: the phase set,
    the amplitude jitter, and the overall elongation/rotation. That is what
    removes the regularity the operator noticed -- two cells drawn from the same
    code with different draws are visibly different shapes, not scaled copies.

    ``harmonics`` optionally narrows the set to its lowest orders. That is how the
    no-fly core is built: it must keep the parent's asymmetry but must not carry
    detail that scaling would amplify into spikes.
```

### L932

```
Simplify, but refuse a result that has lost the shape to over-cutting.

    ``simplify`` is applied to the risk tiers so their vertex counts stay modest
    when they are shipped to the browser. It is a lossy operation, and the loss
    is not bounded by the tolerance in a way that matters here: on a small
    irregular outline a tolerance that looks proportional can still flatten the
    whole silhouette, because the turns it removes are exactly the turns that
    carry the asymmetry the operator asked to see ("大小不一、不规则").

    So the simplified result is only accepted if it still has enough vertices to
    describe a closed irregular curve. When it does not, the original is
    returned. Redundant vertices cost a few bytes on the wire; a core rendered as
    a hexagon costs the feature its meaning.
```

### L960

```
Largest turn between consecutive edges of a closed ring, in radians.

    This is the quantity OUTLINE_MAX_TURN_RAD limits, measured rather than
    bounded. It is the exterior angle at each vertex, so a smooth circle of n
    vertices reports 2*pi/n and anything far above that is a cusp.

    Used by ``_irregular_outline`` to refine its vertex count: the analytic
    budget there bounds the radius slope, which is not the same thing as the
    turn, so the count has to be checked against the real angle. Measuring the
    turn is strictly better than modelling it here -- the cost is one pass over
    the vertices that were just built, and it cannot be wrong about the geometry
    it is looking at.
```

### L993

```
The crossable band around a cell's core.

    Built as *two* concentric low-order outlines -- one at the core's radius, one
    at that radius plus ``buffer_m`` -- rather than as ``field.buffer(900)`` minus
    a core. Both edges then share the same harmonics and are concentric by
    construction, so their difference is an annulus of even width.

    The version this replaces mixed a buffered *detailed* outline with a low-order
    core. Two nearly-parallel smooth curves cross repeatedly, and the difference
    operator keeps every sliver between the crossings: measured 177.4 and 166.0
    degree corners on one cell, which is a zero-width spike that would render as a
    hairline across the map and has no meaning as airspace.

    Both tiers are shifted by the same ``CORE_OFFSET_M`` the core uses, so the
    inner edge of the ring and the outer edge of the core are the same curve and
    the two tiers tile without overlapping or leaving a gap.
```

### L1031

```
The no-fly core: a smaller, smoother copy of a cell's own outline.

    Two constraints have to hold at once, and the first version satisfied only
    one of them.

    It must be a *scaled copy* of the same outline rather than a buffered shrink.
    ``buffer()`` on a lumpy outline wraps a round join around every lump and
    exploded 24 vertices into ~215 of sawtooth, which is what made the ban look
    frayed.

    But scaling the *high-detail* outline is also wrong, and that is the defect
    this function fixes: scaling multiplies the high-frequency excursions too, so
    the field's fine roughness becomes a ring of spikes on the core. Measured on
    seed 42 at minute 60: 60 exterior turns above 45 degrees, worst 141.8, with
    adjacent edges as short as 90 m on a ~9 km cell.

    The core is therefore rebuilt from only the *lowest-order* harmonics of the
    same shape, so it keeps the parent's overall asymmetry -- it is visibly the
    same cloud -- while dropping the detail that scaling would turn into spikes.

    The rebuild is done at the cell's **own live radius**, not at unit radius.
    That is not a detail: an earlier version built the smooth outline at radius
    1.0 and scaled it by 0.58, which produced a 0.58 m disc. Shapely's ``buffer(0)``
    then collapsed it to a degenerate 4-vertex polygon of zero area with ~1 m
    edges, and the test that walks the core's exterior turns read those right
    angles as 150+ degree sharp corners. The geometry was near-empty; only the
    measurement made it look like a shape.

    The offset keeps the core from being perfectly concentric, which would read as
    a drawn ring rather than a derived core. It is a fixed few hundred metres,
    small relative to any cell, so it cannot itself introduce a corner.
```

### L1073

```
Discrete clusters along the band centreline, with usable gaps between them.

    Returns ``(clusters, anchor_point)`` where ``clusters`` is a list of per-cluster
    records, each carrying its own centre, base radius, outline parameters and life
    cycle. The chain is *not* pre-unioned into one polygon any more, and that change
    is what makes the band evolve.

    While the chain was a single stored MultiPolygon the only motion available was
    to translate all of it by one offset, so ``shapes(t)`` had nothing to vary
    except position: measured, ``radius_at`` returned a constant 30000 m at every
    minute and the cell kept exactly 20 polygon parts from t=0 to t=470. A front
    that cannot gain or lose segments cannot be said to have passed. Keeping the
    clusters separate lets each one run ``BAND_CLUSTER_LIFE_MINUTES`` on its own
    stagger, so members decay behind the line and are replaced ahead of it.

    Cluster centres are sampled along the projected centreline at
    ``BAND_CLUSTER_SPACING_M``; radii cycle through ``BAND_CLUSTER_RADII_M`` so the
    chain reads as large cloud masses with smaller ones between them, the way a
    front looks, instead of a ruler-straight ribbon.

    The gaps are the whole point and are enforced by construction rather than hoped
    for: the spacing is kept wider than ``r[i] + r[i+1] + 2*BAND_CLUSTER_GAP_M`` for
    the largest adjacent pair the cycle can produce, so no two clusters can touch and
    every gap stays wide enough to fly through. See ``BAND_CLUSTER_RADII_M`` for the
    measured reason satellites were removed.

    The end clusters are shortened rather than dropped when they land on a provincial
    capital: a cap sitting on top of a capital seals the last corridor out of it,
    which is the same unreachable-network failure as the continuous band, just
    localised to one city.

    A lon/lat centreline is used, never a hand-typed metre polygon: the first
    version of this geometry carried raw northing/easting that never went through
    ``weather.geometry.project``, so it sat ~4300 km north of the map. Clipping it
    to the boundary produced an empty field (perfectly valid, and therefore silent),
    the band never rendered, and ``swept()`` still enveloped it and dragged the
    routing envelope's bounding box out to a polygon nowhere near the network.
```

### L1171

```
Radius of one band cluster at minute ``t``, or 0.0 when it is not there.

    The same inverted-parabola life cycle the point cells use (see
    ``NetworkWeather.radius_at``), applied to a frontal *segment* rather than to a
    convective cell. Two differences are deliberate:

    * the scale spans ``BAND_CLUSTER_RADIUS_SCALE`` rather than ``0..1``, because a
      segment of a front does not grow from nothing and vanish to nothing -- it
      thickens, thins, and is then dropped;
    * a cluster below ``BAND_CLUSTER_MIN_RADIUS_M`` returns 0.0 and is dropped by
      ``shapes()``, which is how the chain loses members behind the front instead of
      leaving a trail of slivers.
```

### L1197

```
``(field_outline, core_outline)`` for one cluster, or ``(None, None)``.

    Returns the *drawable* pair at minute ``t``: the full outline at the current
    radius, and the no-fly core built from the same low-order harmonics at the
    inset radius. Sharing the outline parameters is what keeps a growing cluster's
    core concentric with its field -- the two are the same shape at two scales, so
    the difference between them has no crossing to leave a sliver at.

    Cached, because this is a *pure* function of the cluster's stored parameters
    and the scalar minute, and the sweeps ask for the same pairs over and over.
    Profiling the plan attributed 200 s of 358 s to this function: 321,340 calls,
    each rebuilding a 64-vertex outline through the curvature-repair loop in
    ``_sample`` / ``_worst_exterior_turn``. Every one of those calls was
    redundant -- the sweeps only ever use integer minutes (``tick * 5``), so the
    whole key space is about 20 clusters x 97 ticks, while the call count is an
    order of magnitude larger because each of 1116 windows re-asks for the same
    ticks.

    The memo is keyed on the cluster's *identity* and the minute, never on the
    cluster dict: ``lru_cache`` hashes every argument, and a dict is unhashable,
    so putting the cluster in the cached signature raises
    ``TypeError: unhashable type: 'dict'`` on the first call. The identity used
    is ``id(cluster)``, which is safe here because the clusters are built once in
    ``NetworkWeather.__init__`` and kept alive by ``self.cells`` for the lifetime
    of the object, so no id can be recycled underneath the memo. The memo is
    cleared by :meth:`NetworkWeather._forget_outlines` whenever a fresh network
    is built, so two networks never share entries.
```

### L1235

```
Drop every cached band outline.

    Called from ``NetworkWeather.__init__``. The memo is keyed on ``id(cluster)``,
    which is only sound while the objects it refers to are alive: once a network
    is garbage collected its cluster dicts are freed, a later network can be
    allocated at the same addresses, and the stale ``id`` would then resolve to
    the *new* cluster's dict while the cached outline still belonged to the old
    one. Clearing on construction removes that whole class of aliasing.
```

### L1259

```
Body of :func:`_band_cluster_outlines`; see there for why it is cached.

    ``cluster_key`` is ``id(cluster)`` and participates only in the cache key --
    the actual dict is looked up in ``_OUTLINE_CLUSTERS`` so that this signature
    stays fully hashable.
```

### L1285

```
Hull vertices of one cluster's field outline at minute ``t``.

    Memoised on ``(id(cluster), t)`` for the same reason
    :func:`_band_cluster_outlines` is: the sweeps ask for the same pairs from many
    different windows. Profiling put ``_outline_hull_coords`` at 108,476 calls and
    12.5 s of self time, and every call re-hulled a 64-vertex polygon that had
    already been hulled for that same cluster and minute by an earlier window --
    hulling is not free, and the input is identical.

    Returns ``[]`` when the cluster is not alive at ``t``, which is what the
    previous inline ``if outline is None: continue`` achieved.
```

### L1790

```
The observation's visibility in metres, or ``None``.

    Two providers, two shapes: the Open-Meteo adapter publishes the hourly
    ``visibility`` series, the QWeather adapter publishes a single scalar
    ``visibilityM``. Both are read here so a threshold can never depend on
    which provider happened to answer -- the drift that produced a risk cell
    from one source and not the other.
```

### L1806

```
The extreme condition a point observation states, or ``None``.

    ``None`` is the common case and it is the point of this function. See
    "--- What the point feed is allowed to turn into geometry" above for the
    measured cost of the version that
    returned a name for every mildly adverse city.

    The condition code is read from ``conditionClass``, which the adapter fills
    in by mapping **its own** code space (WMO 4677 for Open-Meteo, and风/和风
    icon codes for the alternate provider) onto one vocabulary. Reading
    ``weatherCode`` directly is what this avoids: the two providers publish
    colliding integers for different weather, so a shared numeric set would
    silently read one condition as another -- see ``weather_sources``. A payload
    with no ``conditionClass`` is one whose adapter did not classify it, and the
    numeric thresholds below are then the only evidence, which is the honest
    reading rather than a guess.

    Order matters only for the label: a thunderstorm that also gusts hard is
    reported as the thunderstorm, because that is the condition that puts the
    airspace out of use.
```

### L1843

```
Per-city ``(members, field, ban, caution)`` rows from a real point snapshot.

    This is the *single* place the point-observation thresholds are applied. Both
    the renderable contour (:class:`PublicWeather`) and the routing envelope
    (:class:`LiveWeatherEnvelope`) read it, so a threshold can never drift between
    what the map draws and what the planner avoids -- the split that previously
    let a whole rain band be missed.

    Only an **extreme** observation produces geometry, and that geometry has
    exactly one tier: ``field == ban == caution-free``. Two properties follow, and
    both are load-bearing:

      * ``caution`` is always empty. A "care is advised" reading must not become
        an area the planner refuses to enter; the measured cost of that confusion
        is in the comment above ``EXTREME_WIND_GUST_MPS``.
      * ``field == ban`` states one enforcement level honestly. A station reading
        does not resolve the inside of a thunderstorm from its edge, so inventing
        a graduated ring around it would be a statement about a structure the
        source never measured.

    Thresholds are project flight-risk parameters, deliberately exposed as derived
    rules rather than presented as official warnings.
```

### L1908

```
Risk contours derived from the same public snapshot shown to operators.

    These are system flight-risk zones, not official airspace closures and not
    radar-resolution storm contours. The snapshot metadata remains attached to
    every response so stale or partial coverage cannot be mistaken for real-time
    observations.
```

### L1984

```
Planner-side avoidance geometry built from a real point snapshot.

    ``simulate()`` needs ``envelope(start, end)`` and nothing else: one geometry
    that a flight crossing the window must not enter. It deliberately has *no*
    ``frame()`` and *no* timeline, because a point observation is one measurement
    of one moment -- projecting it across a whole eight-hour plan would be a
    synthesis claim this class is not entitled to make. The map therefore draws
    the real contours from :class:`PublicWeather` (which carries the same
    provenance back to the operator), while this class only answers the routing
    question a conservative planner has to answer.

    The conservative reading is the point: an observation that is only
    *cautious* earns a wider ring here than the 12 km disc the map draws, because
    the cost of routing a flight into a cell the station did not sample is
    higher than the cost of a slightly longer detour.

    ``snapshot`` may be ``None`` (nothing fetched) in which case the envelope is
    empty and the caller must handle the resulting "no weather path" honestly --
    it must never be papered over with synthesised geometry.
```

### L1377

```
A daughter cell separating from ``parent``.

        Observed cell splitting produces two masses that then move apart, and the
        daughter is smaller than the parent. The daughter is given:
          * a birth at a fixed fraction of the parent's life (near maturity);
          * a lifetime of its own, shorter than the parent's;
          * an offset along the parent's own movement axis, so the pair visibly
            separates along the direction the system is travelling rather than
            at a random angle;
          * a slightly different wind vector, so the separation grows with time
            instead of freezing at a fixed distance.
```

### L1416

```
Centre of a cell at timeline minute ``t``.

        The only place cell motion is expressed. ``shapes`` and ``swept`` both
        call it so a rendered risk outline and the envelope that routing avoids
        can never disagree about where the cell is.
```

### L1430

```
Radius of a cell at minute ``t``, following its life cycle.

        A convective cell is not a permanent object of fixed size. The observed
        life history is the classic three-stage one -- towering cumulus, mature,
        dissipating -- with a total lifetime on the order of 45-60 minutes for an
        ordinary cell (Byers & Braham 1949; COMET *Weather Radar Fundamentals*,
        CMA training). The area/thresholded-core growth curve over that life is
        statistically an *inverted parabola*: it grows, peaks, then decays,
        independent of how long the life is (KIT/DWD LifeCycle project,
        2011-2016 radar tracking). That is the shape implemented here.

        A parabola is used rather than an S-curve because the parabola is what
        the radar statistics actually show, and because the sharp fall-off at the
        end is what makes cells visibly *vanish* instead of lingering.

        ``t`` is the timeline minute for the whole run, while a cell's life is
        anchored at ``birth``. Cells whose life has ended return a radius below
        ``MIN_VISIBLE_RADIUS_M``; callers drop them, so the map gains and loses
        cells over the eight-hour run exactly as a real radar loop does.
```

### L1642

```
Envelope of a fixed-outline cell.

        A buffered centre track would be badly wrong here: the band is ~190 km
        across, so buffering its track to contain the outline would produce a
        ~240 km disc that covers most of the network. The convex hull of the
        sampled outlines contains every sampled position and stays close to the
        band's own long, thin shape.

        Rejected: a Minkowski-sum shortcut. Because the sweep set is
        ``{outline + drift(t)}`` and ``drift`` only translates, hulling the drift
        offsets and unioning that with the outline hull looks equivalent and is
        ~60x cheaper. It is NOT -- the drift arc is far from convex (92 of 97
        samples fall strictly inside their own hull), so the shortcut returned a
        hull that was too *small*: measured 121,638,561,897 m^2 against the true
        174,724,291,628 m^2 on a 96-tick window, i.e. 53,085,729,730 m^2 of real
        swept area missing. A too-small envelope is the exact defect that let
        trajectories thread the rain band before it became a first-class cell, so
        the hull is built from every shifted vertex.
```

### L1664

```
Convex hull of the band outline over an exact tick window.

        The outline is fixed and ``drift`` only translates it, so the swept set is
        ``{outline + d_t}`` over the window's ticks. The hull of that set is the
        hull of the outline translates at the offsets ``d_t`` that are *themselves*
        on the hull of the drift path -- an offset strictly inside the drift hull
        contributes a translate that is strictly inside the result, so it cannot
        add an extremal point.

        That is exact, not a sampling approximation, and it is what makes this
        affordable: a 97-tick window keeps only the drift offsets that are on the
        drift hull (5 of 97), so the expensive step -- extending 64 outline
        vertices per tick -- runs 5 times instead of 97 while the drift hull over
        97 points is trivial. Sampling ticks with a *fixed stride* was rejected as
        unsafe: stride 2 already loses 303,023 m^2 on window (0,36) and stride 5
        loses 21,694,389 m^2, and a hull that is too small lets trajectories
        through.

        The whole window's hull is then cached (``_swept_window``), so in practice
        the prune and the hull both run once per distinct tick window -- 1035 times
        per plan rather than 13,419.
```

### L1923

```
``point_risk_shapes`` 的结果，**每个实例只算一次**。

        这是本次构建里最大的一处纯粹浪费。`envelope(start, end)` 对点观测根本不用
        `start`/`end` —— 一次观测就是一次观测 —— 可它原来每次调用都从快照重建一遍
        同样的几何。实测（2026-10-03，虚拟构建，seed 42，7760 班）：
        `envelope` 被调用 **36,309 次**（每班 × 每个等待偏移），累计 5.7 s，
        `point_risk_shapes` 又占 4.8 s，而它们每一次返回的都是**同一份几何**。

        快照在实例的整个生命周期里是只读的（`simulate()` 建一次、整轮构建共用一个），
        所以缓存它不改变任何一个判据 —— 省掉的只是把同一份几何算三万六千遍。
```

### L1941

```
The prohibition geometry a trajectory must not enter -- nothing else.

        Same contract as :meth:`LiveWeatherEnvelope.envelope`, and the same
        measured reason for writing it out explicitly instead of unioning the
        tiers: a caution ring is advice, an obstacle is a refusal, and collapsing
        the two closed 2488 flights whose own pad sat inside a "care advised"
        disc (2026-09-30, live snapshot, seed 42).

        `start`/`end` **刻意不参与**：点观测没有时间维度，一次观测就是一次观测。
        既然入参不影响结果，整块几何（含那句 `unary_union`）就只算一次 ——
        这也是本轮的提速点，理由与实测数字见 `_risk_shapes`。口径一个字没改。
```

### L2017

```
与 :meth:`PublicWeather._risk_shapes` 同一件事、同一个理由。

        这里是虚拟/实测构建真正走的那条路：`_radar_avoidance` 逐班逐偏移调
        `envelope()`，而它每次都重建同一份点位几何。缓存它不改变任何判据 ——
        快照在整轮构建里只读，见那边的实测数字。
```

### L2030

```
The prohibition geometry a trajectory must not enter -- nothing else.

        Written as ``ban`` only, on purpose, and this is the fix for a measured
        defect rather than a tidy-up. The expression used to be
        ``ban.union(caution)``, which put the caution tier into the planner's
        obstacle set; ``routing.route()`` refuses outright when a flight's own
        start or end point is inside that set, so a city with a merely gusty
        reading had every departure closed. The map's own legend and the renderer
        both state the contract the other way round -- "橙黄外圈＝谨慎进入，红色内核
        ＝禁止进入", and ``routing`` only refuses the red one -- so the code, the
        legend and the intent now agree.

        ``caution`` is empty for every point-derived cell anyway (see
        ``point_risk_shapes``); this stays explicit so a future tier cannot
        silently re-enter the obstacle set.
```

```

# Counts how many frames each branch has served since process start. This is
# the evidence that a mode change actually took effect: an operator who
# switches to virtual and sees no movement here is looking at a cache.

# 数据最多只污染自己那一块屏，不会改到别人正在看的实测场。
```

# 免得刚中继完就因为过期被丢弃又去要一次；又不至于长到让屏上出现"看起来是实时
# 但其实是十分钟前"的图。
```

### L83
